Nothing really stands out to me that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t remember ever having a concussion or any serious head injury.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes besides your memory, like difficulty with planning, organizing, or completing tasks, or any changes in your mood or behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve noticed it more with organizing and figuring out what to do first. I mean, I get frustrated when I fall behind on things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad or losing interest in things I normally like.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble handling everyday tasks like cooking, managing finances, or following a recipe that you used to manage easily before?\n\nPatient: Not so much cooking or bills, but everyday things, yeah. What finally made me ask for help was I kept forgetting small stuff I usually handle—like returning calls, turning in a registration form, or remembering to stop for an errand on the way home. appointments).\n  manifestation: What finally made me ask for help is that I keep forgetting little things I normally handle, like returning calls, turning in a registration form, or stopping for an errand on the way home.\n- key: S010\n  name: Fidgeting\n  description: Often fidgets with or taps hands or feet or squirms in seat.\n  manifestation: During longer classes I notice myself clicking my pen, bouncing my foot, or shifting around in the chair even when I’m trying to sit still and pay attention.\n- key: S011\n  name: Leaving_Seat\n  description: Often leaves seat in situations when remaining seated is expected.\n  manifestation: In the three-hour evening sessions, I’ll find reasons to get up for water or step into the hallway for a minute, not to be disruptive but because chance at moving into an Office Manager role.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: These attention and organization problems have been noticeably I can get kind of antsy if I’m sitting too long, but I wouldn’t say I feel like I have to constantly be doing something.\n\nDoctor: Looking back, did you often feel very restless or have trouble staying still during childhood, even when you were not under pressure to focus, like at home or during playtime?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t remember being especially restless at home or during playtime. I think I could sit and play if I was interested in something. Nothing really stands out to me that way.\n\nDoctor: In your adult life, before starting your evening classes, did you often find yourself struggling to finish tasks, avoiding things that require sustained mental effort, or losing focus easily—even in situations where there wasn’t a lot of pressure or deadlines?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I don’t remember ever having a concussion or any serious head injury.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any other changes besides your memory, like difficulty with planning, organizing, or completing tasks, or any changes in your mood or behavior?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’ve noticed it more with organizing and figuring out what to do first. I mean, I get frustrated when I fall behind on things, but I wouldn’t say I’ve been feeling sad or losing interest in things I normally like.\n\nDoctor: Have you had trouble handling everyday tasks like cooking, managing finances, or following a recipe that you used to manage easily before?\n\nPatient: Not so much cooking or bills, but everyday things, yeah. I’m still getting by, but I’ve missed some small follow-ups at work and a few school deadlines. Not huge things, but enough that I’m worried people will start seeing me as unreliable. I’d really like to move toward an Office Manager role, and I’m afraid this could hurt my chances.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your ability to make decisions, solve problems, or stay focused on a task compared to before?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly with staying focused. I don’t think my decision-making is that different, but at the office or in class, it doesn’t take much to throw me off. I end up spending time just trying to figure out what I’m supposed to do first instead of actually doing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you were a child, did you have trouble staying focused or organizing tasks in more than just school—like at home or with friends—or was it mostly in school settings?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / secretary_or_administrative_assistant / high_school\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Even in elementary school, I was the kid who forgot permission slips, lost homework in my backpack, and needed my mother or teacher to remind me step by step to finish projects.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It wasn’t only school. Even in elementary school, I’d forget permission slips or lose homework somewhere in my backpack. At home, my mom had to remind me step by step to finish projects, and teachers did that too. So it was kind of the same pattern in more than one place.

I can start things, but when there’s a lot at once I don’t always know what to tackle first. Since then the focus and organizing stuff has been harder to keep up with.\n\nDoctor: Do these issues with focus and organization happen in different places—like at work, at home, and during your classes—or are they limited to just one setting, like when you're at work or school?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to separate it out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.
